Lately I open one and instead of feeling curious or comforted, it just makes me feel heavy. Like I can see that it should matter to me, but I can’t quite feel it the way I used to. It’s a very discouraging feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling as if you are to blame for things that really aren’t your fault?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do feel bad about things, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m blaming myself for things that aren’t my fault exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I second-guess myself a lot. If something doesn’t get done right, or if I’m slower than I should be, I’ll turn it over in my head and think, “Well, maybe I should’ve handled that better.” But I don’t know that I’m taking responsibility for, say, things that clearly had nothing to do with me. I haven’t noticed that kind of thought in a big way.\n\nI suppose I can be hard on myself, but I’m not sure that’s the same thing as what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have done something terribly wrong or caused harm, even when other people tell you that you have not?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I can feel guilty about things, and I can replay conversations in my head more than is probably useful, but I don’t have this fixed belief that I’ve done some terrible harm and everyone else is just trying to reassure me.\n\nSometimes if someone says, “No, it’s fine,” I’ll still wonder if they’re just being polite, especially if I feel like I was careless or short with them. But that’s more ordinary worrying, I think. I don’t sit there believing I’ve ruined someone’s life or caused something awful when people are telling me I haven’t.\n\nSo… no, not in that strong way. I can be self-critical, but I don’t think I’m convinced I’ve done something terrible when I haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt convinced that something inside your body has stopped working or that parts of you are dead or rotting?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t think I’ve felt anything like that. I mean, I’ve had times where my body feels tired or heavy, and I can get kind of preoccupied with how worn down I feel, but not in the sense that I believe something has stopped working or that part of me is dead or rotting.\n\nThat sounds very frightening, honestly, but it’s not something I’ve noticed in myself. I haven’t been thinking my organs aren’t working or that my body is decaying or anything along those lines.\n\nDoctor: Has your thinking gotten worse in sudden steps, such as after a stroke or similar medical event?\n\nPatient: Not like a sudden step after a stroke or something, no… at least, not that I can point to that way. It’s more that I came in because my mind feels foggy, and it’s been bothering me enough that I can’t just brush it off.\n\nAt work, I’ve done clerical library things for years, you know, old cataloguing notes and little routines that used to be almost automatic. Now I’ll be looking at my own notes and lose my place, like I can’t keep the thread of what I was doing. I’ll read the same line and then think, “Wait, did I already do that part, or was I about to do it?” And then even deciding the next simple thing—something that shouldn’t require much thought—can feel oddly hard.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as one big sudden change after a medical event. It’s more this fogginess and slowing down in my thinking that made me worried enough to come talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a medical illness or brain problem that could be affecting your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I mean, no doctor has sat me down and said, “You have this illness” or “there’s a brain problem causing your memory or thinking trouble.” At least… not in any clear way like that.\n\nI’ve had doctors ask general questions, you know, routine things, but I haven’t been told there’s some medical explanation for it. That’s partly why I’m here, honestly, because I don’t know what to make of it. I don’t want to assume it’s something physical or something in my brain without someone actually evaluating me, but I also don’t want to ignore it if it matters.\n\nSo the short answer is no — I haven’t been diagnosed with a medical illness or brain condition that I’ve been told is affecting my thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting confused about what day or date it is?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I do check the date sometimes, but I think most people do that, especially when the days feel kind of similar. I haven’t had moments where I wake up and truly don’t know what day it is, or think it’s a completely different date and then find out I’m way off.\n\nSometimes if I’m not paying attention, I might have to look at a calendar or my phone and say, “Oh right, it’s Tuesday,” but it doesn’t feel like confusion exactly. More like I just hadn’t kept track in that moment. So no, I haven’t really noticed getting confused about the day or date in a way that feels alarming.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. It’s not like I suddenly decided I don’t like things anymore, but most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring at nothing, and it feels like the color has gone out of everything.\n\nI used to really like looking through my old watercolor books — not even necessarily painting, just paging through them, looking at the colors and the techniques and feeling a little spark of interest. But lately I’ll take one down and open it, and instead of feeling pulled in, it just feels heavy. Like even the thing that used to be comforting now takes effort, and I can’t quite get pleasure from it the way I used to.\n\nSo, yes… I’d say I’ve lost a lot of interest, or at least the pleasure has gone very flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if other people would notice it right away, but I notice it. My body just feels slow, like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nEven walking to the corner store feels like this whole production sometimes — getting my shoes on, stepping outside, actually making it there. It’s not that it’s far, it’s just… I feel like I’m moving through something thick. Maybe I pause more than usual, or take longer to get words out. But the moving part, yes. I’ve definitely felt slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a stroke or a mini-stroke?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never been told I had a stroke or one of those mini-strokes.\n\nI mean, I’ve heard people talk about them, and I know sometimes people can have something small and not realize right away, so I don’t want to sound like I’m being overly certain about things I wouldn’t necessarily know. I’ve never been told I have Parkinson’s or MS or lupus, or anything like that. I mean, I’ve had times where my body feels tired or heavy, and I can get kind of preoccupied with how worn down I feel, but not in the sense that I believe something has stopped working or that part of me is dead or rotting.\n\nThat sounds very frightening, honestly, but it’s not something I’ve noticed in myself. If anything, I might worry in a vague way when I don’t feel right, but I don’t have a conviction like that. I haven’t been thinking my organs aren’t working or that my body is decaying or anything along those lines.\n\nDoctor: Has your thinking gotten worse in sudden steps, such as after a stroke or similar medical event?\n\nPatient: Not like a sudden step after a stroke or something, no… at least, not that I can point to that way. It’s more that I came in because my mind feels foggy, and it’s been bothering me enough that I can’t just brush it off.\n\nAt work, I’ve done clerical library things for years, you know, old cataloguing notes and little routines that used to be almost automatic. Now I’ll be looking at my own notes and lose my place, like I can’t keep the thread of what I was doing. I’ll read the same line and then think, “Wait, did I already do that part, or was I about to do it?” And then even deciding the next simple thing—something that shouldn’t require much thought—can feel oddly hard.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as one big sudden change after a medical event. It’s more this fogginess and slowing down in my thinking that made me worried enough to come talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a medical illness or brain problem that could be affecting your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I mean, no doctor has sat me down and said, “You have this illness” or “there’s a brain problem causing your memory or thinking trouble.” At least… not in any clear way like that.\n\nI’ve had doctors ask general questions, you know, routine things, but I haven’t been told there’s some medical explanation for it. That’s partly why I’m here, honestly, because I don’t know what to make of it. I don’t want to assume it’s something physical or something in my brain without someone actually evaluating me, but I also don’t want to ignore it if it matters.\n\nSo the short answer is no — I haven’t been diagnosed with a medical illness or brain condition that I’ve been told is affecting my thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting confused about what day or date it is?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I do check the date sometimes, but I think most people do that, especially when the days feel kind of similar. I haven’t had moments where I wake up and truly don’t know what day it is, or think it’s a completely different date and then find out I’m way off.\n\nSometimes if I’m not paying attention, I might have to look at a calendar or my phone and say, “Oh right, it’s Tuesday,” but it doesn’t feel like confusion exactly. More like I just hadn’t kept track in that moment. So no, I haven’t really noticed getting confused about the day or date in a way that feels alarming.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. It’s not like I suddenly decided I don’t like things anymore, but most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring at nothing, and it feels like the color has gone out of everything.\n\nI used to really like looking through my old watercolor books — not even necessarily painting, just paging through them, looking at the colors and the techniques and feeling a little spark of interest. But lately I’ll take one down and open it, and instead of feeling pulled in, it just feels heavy. Like even the thing that used to be comforting now takes effort, and I can’t quite get pleasure from it the way I used to.\n\nSo, yes… I’d say I’ve lost a lot of interest, or at least the pleasure has gone very flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if other people would notice it right away, but I notice it. My body just feels slow, like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nEven walking to the corner store feels like this whole production sometimes — getting my shoes on, stepping outside, actually making it there. It’s not that it’s far, it’s just… I feel like I’m moving through something thick. And at my desk, even sorting papers or straightening things into piles, which should be automatic, feels like I have to push myself through each little step.\n\nSpeaking-wise, I’m not sure. Maybe I pause more than usual, or take longer to get words out. But the moving part, yes. I’ve definitely felt slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a stroke or a mini-stroke?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never been told I had a stroke or one of those mini-strokes.\n\nI mean, I’ve heard people talk about them, and I know sometimes people can have something small and not realize right away, so I don’t want to sound like I’m being overly certain about things I wouldn’t necessarily know. But as far as my own history goes, no doctor has ever said that to me, and I haven’t had anything that I understood as a stroke.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical condition that can affect the brain, such as Parkinson’s disease, multiple sclerosis, lupus, HIV, or epilepsy?\n\nPatient: Not that I’m aware of, no. I’ve never been told I have Parkinson’s or MS or lupus, or anything like that. And I haven’t been diagnosed with HIV or epilepsy either.\n\nI mean, I’ve had the usual doctor visits over the years, and sometimes they ask those kinds of history questions, but no one has ever sat me down and said I have a neurological condition or an autoimmune thing affecting my brain. So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor ever told you that you have vascular dementia or hardening of the arteries in your brain?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe so. Nobody’s ever told me I have vascular dementia, and I don’t remember anyone saying anything like “hardening of the arteries in the brain” to me.\n\nI mean, I’ve had doctors explain things in medical terms before where I don’t catch every single word, so I suppose it’s possible someone used a different phrase and I didn’t understand it that way. But as far as an actual diagnosis, no — I haven’t been told that.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you are, even in familiar places?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I walk into a room and forget what I came in there for, or I’m distracted and it takes me a second to get my bearings, but not like I’m in a familiar place and don’t know where I am.\n\nAt the library where I work, or around my neighborhood, or at home, I know where I am. I haven’t had that experience of looking around and thinking, “Where am I?” in a place I should recognize. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor ever told you that your thyroid is underactive?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t remember a doctor ever specifically saying, “Your thyroid is underactive,” or giving me that as a clear diagnosis.\n\nI’ve had routine blood work before, and sometimes they mention checking this or that, but I can’t say I always remember every lab they’re talking about. If someone had told me it was a major issue, I think I’d remember. So as far as I know, no — I haven’t been told that.\n\nDoctor: Did your trouble with memory and thinking start around the same time as your low mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. That’s actually part of why I came in, because it feels like when my mood dropped, my thinking sort of went with it. My mind feels foggy a lot of the time, like I’m trying to work through cotton or something.\n\nAt work, I’ve been doing the same kind of cataloguing notes for years, and lately I can’t even keep my place in them. She’ll point out that I’ve left rice or soup half-finished, and that’s not really like me. I grew up not wasting food, and normally if there’s a meal in front of me, I’ll finish it without even thinking. Lately I’ll eat a little and then just sort of stop, like I’ve lost interest, and then the bowl is still sitting there.\n\nSo, yes, my appetite is definitely lower than it used to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But that’s more ordinary worrying, I think. I don’t sit there believing I’ve ruined someone’s life or caused something awful when people are telling me I haven’t.\n\nSo… no, not in that strong way. I haven’t been thinking my organs aren’t working or that my body is decaying or anything along those lines.\n\nDoctor: Has your thinking gotten worse in sudden steps, such as after a stroke or similar medical event?\n\nPatient: Not like a sudden step after a stroke or something, no… at least, not that I can point to that way. It’s more this fogginess and slowing down in my thinking that made me worried enough to come talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a medical illness or brain problem that could be affecting your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I mean, no doctor has sat me down and said, “You have this illness” or “there’s a brain problem causing your memory or thinking trouble.” At least… not in any clear way like that.\n\nI’ve had doctors ask general questions, you know, routine things, but I haven’t been told there’s some medical explanation for it. That’s partly why I’m here, honestly, because I don’t know what to make of it. I don’t want to assume it’s something physical or something in my brain without someone actually evaluating me, but I also don’t want to ignore it if it matters.\n\nSo the short answer is no — I haven’t been diagnosed with a medical illness or brain condition that I’ve been told is affecting my thinking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting confused about what day or date it is?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I do check the date sometimes, but I think most people do that, especially when the days feel kind of similar. I haven’t had moments where I wake up and truly don’t know what day it is, or think it’s a completely different date and then find out I’m way off.\n\nSometimes if I’m not paying attention, I might have to look at a calendar or my phone and say, “Oh right, it’s Tuesday,” but it doesn’t feel like confusion exactly. More like I just hadn’t kept track in that moment. So no, I haven’t really noticed getting confused about the day or date in a way that feels alarming.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I have. It’s not like I suddenly decided I don’t like things anymore, but most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, kind of staring at nothing, and it feels like the color has gone out of everything.\n\nI used to really like looking through my old watercolor books — not even necessarily painting, just paging through them, looking at the colors and the techniques and feeling a little spark of interest. But lately I’ll take one down and open it, and instead of feeling pulled in, it just feels heavy. Like even the thing that used to be comforting now takes effort, and I can’t quite get pleasure from it the way I used to.\n\nSo, yes… I’d say I’ve lost a lot of interest, or at least the pleasure has gone very flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking noticeably more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if other people would notice it right away, but I notice it. My body just feels slow, like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nEven walking to the corner store feels like this whole production sometimes — getting my shoes on, stepping outside, actually making it there. It’s not that it’s far, it’s just… I feel like I’m moving through something thick. And at my desk, even sorting papers or straightening things into piles, which should be automatic, feels like I have to push myself through each little step.\n\nSpeaking-wise, I’m not sure. Maybe I pause more than usual, or take longer to get words out. But the moving part, yes. I’ve definitely felt slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a stroke or a mini-stroke?\n\nPatient: No… not that I know of. I’ve never been told I had a stroke or one of those mini-strokes.\n\nI mean, I’ve heard people talk about them, and I know sometimes people can have something small and not realize right away, so I don’t want to sound like I’m being overly certain about things I wouldn’t necessarily know. But as far as my own history goes, no doctor has ever said that to me, and I haven’t had anything that I understood as a stroke.\n\nDoctor: Have you been diagnosed with any medical condition that can affect the brain, such as Parkinson’s disease, multiple sclerosis, lupus, HIV, or epilepsy?\n\nPatient: Not that I’m aware of, no. I’ve never been told I have Parkinson’s or MS or lupus, or anything like that. And I haven’t been diagnosed with HIV or epilepsy either.\n\nI mean, I’ve had the usual doctor visits over the years, and sometimes they ask those kinds of history questions, but no one has ever sat me down and said I have a neurological condition or an autoimmune thing affecting my brain. So as far as I know, no.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor ever told you that you have vascular dementia or hardening of the arteries in your brain?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t believe so. Nobody’s ever told me I have vascular dementia, and I don’t remember anyone saying anything like “hardening of the arteries in the brain” to me.\n\nI mean, I’ve had doctors explain things in medical terms before where I don’t catch every single word, so I suppose it’s possible someone used a different phrase and I didn’t understand it that way. But as far as an actual diagnosis, no — I haven’t been told that.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten confused about where you are, even in familiar places?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I walk into a room and forget what I came in there for, or I’m distracted and it takes me a second to get my bearings, but not like I’m in a familiar place and don’t know where I am.\n\nAt the library where I work, or around my neighborhood, or at home, I know where I am. I haven’t had that experience of looking around and thinking, “Where am I?” in a place I should recognize. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor ever told you that your thyroid is underactive?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t remember a doctor ever specifically saying, “Your thyroid is underactive,” or giving me that as a clear diagnosis.\n\nI’ve had routine blood work before, and sometimes they mention checking this or that, but I can’t say I always remember every lab they’re talking about. If someone had told me it was a major issue, I think I’d remember. So as far as I know, no — I haven’t been told that.\n\nDoctor: Did your trouble with memory and thinking start around the same time as your low mood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. That’s actually part of why I came in, because it feels like when my mood dropped, my thinking sort of went with it. My mind feels foggy a lot of the time, like I’m trying to work through cotton or something.\n\nAt work, I’ve been doing the same kind of cataloguing notes for years, and lately I can’t even keep my place in them. I’ll be looking at an old note or a record and then I lose the thread of what I was doing, and I have to start over. And sometimes it’s not even a complicated decision — it’s just deciding what simple thing to do next — and I’ll sit there feeling stuck. So, yes, it seems tied together with the low mood, at least from how it feels to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a big change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yes, there has been a change. I don’t feel hungry the way I used to. It’s not like I’m refusing food on purpose or trying to cut back or anything — I just… don’t think about eating unless someone reminds me.\n\nMy wife has noticed it more than I have, honestly. She’ll point out that I’ve left rice or soup half-finished, and that’s not really like me. I grew up not wasting food, and normally if there’s a meal in front of me, I’ll finish it without even thinking. Lately I’ll eat a little and then just sort of stop, like I’ve lost interest, and then the bowl is still sitting there.\n\nSo, yes, my appetite is definitely lower than it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have these low mood and thinking problems been present most days for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes — definitely longer than two weeks. It’s been most days for, I’d say, about three months now.\n\nIt kind of started around when I was getting ready to leave the library, or preparing for that change, and at first I thought maybe it was just the stress of it or being tired. But it didn’t really lift. The low mood has been there almost every day, and the fogginess too — like they arrived together and just stayed. Some days are a little better than others, but I can’t say there have been many days where I’ve felt like my usual self.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
